It just hasn't stopped.\n\nDoctor: How long ago did your friend pass away?\n\nPatient: It was... about two years and three months ago. We were on a walking tour when... when it happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of what happened to your friend?\n\nPatient: Yes. I... I don't go near that intersection anymore. I just go out of my way to avoid it entirely. And... I used to do a lot of tours, but I stopped signing up for them. Safety meetings, too. I just... I can't sit there and listen to people talk about things that... that remind me of it. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling on edge or easily startled since the incident?\n\nPatient: I suppose so... yes. Mostly when I have to be near traffic. I just... I find myself watching every truck. Every curb, every pedestrian. It's like... I feel like it's my responsibility now. To catch the danger before it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend’s death, have you found yourself feeling consistently guilty, angry, or emotionally numb?\n\nPatient: Yes... I suppose so. I guess... that's really why I agreed to come in. I've just been living with this constant... mix of guilt. And fear. Anger, too, ever since she was killed. \n\nEveryone seemed to think it would fade over time, but... it hasn't eased at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a lasting sense of guilt or anger since your friend's death, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess... yes. I don't really do much of what I used to. I had my garden beds to plan, and... quilting with the ladies. Theater outings. Book club... \n\nBut now, I just look at those things on the calendar and... I don't know. I feel almost nothing. It's like... like those things belong to someone I used to be. I just don't have it in me right now.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings of guilt, anger, or emotional numbness been present nearly every day for most of the day, and have they lasted continuously for more than two months?\n\nPatient: Yes... I suppose it's every day. And it's been much longer than two months. \n\nIt's been like this ever since she was killed on that walking tour. That was... about two years and three months ago now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these emotions—guilt, anger, or numbness—are always with you, even when nothing specific is happening to remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really know. I haven't thought about it quite like that. I haven't really paid attention to whether something specific is happening or not... I'm just not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty, angry, or emotionally numb even when you’re not reminded of your friend’s death by something in your surroundings?\n\nPatient: Like I just said... I really couldn't tell you. I don't keep track of what's around me when I feel that way. I just... I don't have an answer for that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you ever feel guilt, anger, or emotional numbness even when you're not thinking about your friend or anything related to the incident?\n\nPatient: I suppose... yes. It's just... a constant mix of guilt, fear, and anger. It's been there ever since she was killed. I thought it would ease up over time, like everyone said it would, but... it hasn't. It's just always there.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a time when you felt guilt, anger, or numbness without being reminded of your friend's death by something in your surroundings or your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I... I can't really point to one specific day or moment. I don't keep track of it like that. It's just a heavy feeling that doesn't really leave. I don't know how else to explain it to you.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt guilt, anger, or emotional numbness even when you weren’t thinking about your friend or anything connected to the incident?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. Like I said, it's just always there. I'm not... I'm not analyzing what I'm thinking about every time I feel it. It's just a constant weight.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember a time when you felt guilt, anger, or emotional numbness even when you weren't reminded of your friend's death by your thoughts or anything around you?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know how else to answer that. It's just the same as I've been saying. I don't keep a log of it. It's just... there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you felt guilt, anger, or emotional numbness even when you weren’t thinking about your friend or anything connected to the incident?\n\nPatient: I... look, doctor, you keep asking me the same thing. I don't know how to separate it out for you. Since my friend was killed, I've just been living with this... this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger. It's just always there. It hasn't eased up the way everyone said it would. I don't know when it's connected to a thought or not anymore. It's just my life now.\n\nDoctor: So, to clarify, do you ever feel guilt, anger, or emotional numbness when you’re not actively thinking about your friend or anything related to the incident?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I... I really don't have a different answer for you. I'm just not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
